It would explain the **confidence and authority** he wielded – because he carefully constructed it. | – **Character and reception don’t align with pure fraud:** This hypothesis struggles to explain Muhammad’s personal sacrifices and the depth of belief of those close to him. If he was simply acting, he maintained the facade impeccably even when it brought great risk (the years of persecution in Mecca, the hunger and trials). It’s difficult to maintain that *all* early believers (many of whom suffered greatly) were duped by a single man’s lie. Additionally, Muhammad’s reputation even among enemies was “truthful” – a pure charlatan starting as a truth-abiding citizen for 40 years with no prior deception record is hard to imagine.<br>– **No “confession” or leak:** With many confidants (his wives, Ali, Abu Bakr, etc.), one might expect if it were all fake that someone close might hint at it in historical records. Instead, sources (even those collected by skeptics) show sincere conviction in the community. A conspiracy on this scale with zero contemporaneous expose undermines the likelihood of a deliberate hoax. It requires assuming extraordinary **cynicism** and acting skill on Muhammad’s part, which is at odds with the reminiscences of his personality (kind, unpretentious in daily life) that even critical historians like W. Montgomery Watt find genuine. | – **If this were true,** it reduces the foundation of Islam to a **human power project**. The moral and social principles of Islam could still be admirable or effective, but their sanctity would be a deliberate construct. That would mean the authority of Islamic law and scripture is human-made. For devout Muslims, this is a deeply offensive and implausible idea, effectively negating the religion’s truth claims. For critics, it would portray Muhammad as an innovative state-builder who cloaked politics in piety – in Marx’s terms, using religion as the “opium of the masses” to achieve unity and obedience.<br>– **In a more charitable version,** one could see it as a *noble lie*: perhaps Muhammad saw the dire need for Arab moral reform and unity, and believed only a God-given law could bind the tribes. So he **consciously adopted the mantle of prophet** to infuse his social program with unassailable legitimacy. In that scenario, his intentions were semi-noble (the ends of peace and monotheism justify the means of a manufactured revelation). However, even this benign spin still implies deception at the core of Islam. It also raises a theological paradox – that a false prophecy produced a major world religion with considerable good in it (something a believer of another faith might reconcile as God using even deception for a grand purpose, whereas a secular person might call it historical accident). | For secular historians, this hypothesis can explain a lot of the **political efficacy** of Muhammad’s work. It views him as a conscious architect of a new social order who skillfully wielded **religious narrative** to motivate and govern. It explains why revelations often had immediate utility and why the movement adapted so quickly to changing circumstances (because its leader was making calculated decisions and dressing them in divine garb). However, it fails to account for the **intangible inspirational aspect** – the genuine devotion and spiritual elevation felt by followers. Movements built purely on manipulation often fragment or collapse once hardships come; Islam, however, thrived even under persecution, suggesting more than just trickery was at play. As an academic stance, a few Western historians in the past leaned toward this (seeing Muhammad as a **clever reformer** who might have used pious fraud initially), but modern scholarship is cautious with such claims given lack of evidence. Explanatory power here is double-edged: it’s rational from a realpolitik angle, but it doesn’t *deeply* explain the fervor and longevity of the faith (one would have to admit that even if originally a “performance,” it took on a life of sincere faith among millions). |

In evaluating these hypotheses, it’s possible that elements of several overlap. For instance, Muhammad could have had genuine altered-state experiences (#2) which he then actively interpreted and leveraged (#4), with followers believing the angel hypothesis (#1). Hypothesis #3 (demonic influence) is usually only entertained within faith-versus-faith polemics and has little traction in objective scholarship. Hypothesis #5 (fraud) remains popular in certain anti-Islamic circles but is generally not supported by professional historians due to the complexity of factors at play and Muhammad’s demonstrated sincerity. The most common secular academic approach might combine #2 and #4: seeing Muhammad as a **charismatic religious personality** who **experienced something profound internally**, then gradually developed his message in interaction with his community’s needs – essentially a **new religious movement leader** in line with comparative examples, without requiring divine or diabolic validation. 

From the **UTC perspective**, one could say Muhammad’s consciousness operated at a high level of complexity: capable of intense **introspective experiences (I-... levels)** and of harnessing those experiences to build a cooperative social network. Whether one views the source of his inspiration as divine or human, the outcome was a powerful **“institutional grammar”** – a set of narratives, symbols, and practices – that proved scalable far beyond Muhammad’s own lifetime. In the next sections, we will see how that grammar, once it included the concept of **sanctified coercion**, enabled an empire to take shape.

## Medina: The Great Hinge (From Movement to Proto-State)

The Hijra of 622 CE – the migration to Yathrib (Medina) – marks a **pivot point** where Muhammad’s role expanded from purely a prophet-reformer to a **political leader and state-builder**. In Medina, the Muslim community (ummah) underwent a dramatic transformation: from a persecuted sect in Mecca to a nascent **polity** with governance structures, alliances, and military defense. This period can aptly be called the “Great Hinge” because it’s where the moral movement had to reconcile its ideals with the practical necessities of administering a society. 

**Constitution of Medina:** One of Muhammad’s first actions in Medina was to establish a **social contract** among its inhabitants, remembered as the **Constitution of Medina (Ṣaḥīfat al-Madīnah)**. This document (dated to 622 CE) is arguably **the first written constitution** in the Arab world[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Medina#:~:text=The%20Constitution%20has%20been%20highly,Note%202). It created a **federation** between the Muslim emigrants from Mecca (Muhājirūn), the newly converted Medinan tribes (Anṣār of Aus and Khazraj), and importantly, the **Jewish tribes** already residing in Yathrib[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Medina#:~:text=This%20constitution%20gave%20the%20Islamic,or). The Constitution declared all Muslims (both emigrants and local converts) to be “one community (ummah) to the exclusion of others,” transcending tribal identities. It also acknowledged the Jewish clans as a community of their own faith but allied in the collective, stating “the Jews have their religion and the Muslims have theirs,” and promising them security as long as they remained loyal[rsis.edu.sg](https://rsis.edu.sg/wp-content/uploads/2016/02/CO16035.pdf#:~:text=,live%20by%20their%20own%20beliefs)[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Medina#:~:text=This%20constitution%20gave%20the%20Islamic,or). In effect, Muhammad was recognized as the **chief arbitrator and leader** of Medina – a stunning change from Mecca, where he had held no formal authority. 

This constitution, while high-minded in guaranteeing **religious pluralism and mutual defense**, also had pragmatic politics at its core. Medina had been wracked by years of blood feuds between the Aws and Khazraj tribes (culminating in the Battle of Bu‘āth shortly before Muhammad’s arrival)[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Medina#:~:text=According%20to%20traditional%20Islamic%20belief%2C,168%20were%20no%20longer). The city’s leaders sought an outsider with moral authority to unify them and prevent civil war. Muhammad fit the bill. In UTC terms, Medina offered a context for **“collaborative competition”** – previously rivalrous sub-systems (tribes) agreed to cooperate under a new framework for a greater good (peace and order). Muhammad’s leadership provided the **supernode** that coordinated this new alliance, reducing destructive competition internally.

**State-building and Resource Base:** Once in power, Muhammad’s administration needed a **resource base** to sustain the community. Unlike Mecca, Medina (an agricultural oasis) had no major trading economy; it was smaller and economically less affluent. The Muslim emigrants, many of whom left behind property in Mecca, were largely destitute upon arrival. Muhammad enacted a brotherhood pact pairing each Meccan emigrant with a Medinan host family (establishing **new kinship bonds by oath**), which helped share resources. But charity and date farming alone could not support the growing community, especially as more converts joined. 

It’s in this context that **raiding emerged as a tactic**. Caravan raiding was a time-honored Arab practice – a form of **low-level warfare cum livelihood** that avoided full-blown battles by targeting trade convoys. Now, with Muhammad as leader, the Muslims began to organize **ghazawāt (expeditions)** against the caravans of the Quraysh. This served **multiple purposes**: economically, it could yield booty (foodstuffs, goods, camels) to feed and finance the community; politically, it put pressure on Mecca by striking its lifeblood trade; and symbolically, it started to **redress injustices**, as the Muslims felt entitled to reclaim wealth since they had been driven out of Mecca. Early raids, like those at Nakhla, were small-scale and sometimes unsuccessful[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=match%20at%20L1368%20Muhammad%20ordered,Muslims%20set%20an%20ambush%20for). In fact, Islamic chronicles say the first few expeditions under Muhammad’s command did not catch their targets – indicating that these were tentative forays, likely to avoid heavy engagement until strength was sufficient[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=match%20at%20L1368%20Muhammad%20ordered,Muslims%20set%20an%20ambush%20for). 

Crucially, Muhammad had **sanction from his new scripture** to engage in these raids. Not long after the Hijra, a Qur’anic revelation came: *“Permission [to fight] is given to those who have been wronged… who have been driven from their homes unjustly only for saying, ‘Our Lord is Allah’...”*[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=been%20evicted%20from%20their%20homes,Powerful%20and%20Exalted%20in%20Might). This verse (Qur’an 22:39–40) explicitly permits the believers to take up arms in defense of their community, framing it as fighting **oppression and preserving religious freedom**[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=been%20evicted%20from%20their%20homes,Powerful%20and%20Exalted%20in%20Might). This was a watershed doctrinal change. In Mecca, no fighting was allowed; in Medina, **sanctified coercion** became part of the program. The moral reasoning was that war was justified to prevent greater harm – the destruction of monasteries, churches, synagogues, and mosques is mentioned in the same passage, suggesting a universal principle of protecting worship and justice[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=been%20evicted%20from%20their%20homes,Powerful%20and%20Exalted%20in%20Might).

From a **governance perspective**, Muhammad also began **administration** in Medina. He served as judge in disputes, arbitraited in accordance with emerging Islamic principles. The Constitution of Medina laid out mechanisms for **blood-money** sharing and collective defense, effectively creating a rudimentary **rule of law** above tribal law[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Medina#:~:text=Most%20historians%20have%20accepted%20the,8)[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Medina#:~:text=aspects%20of%20the%20Constitution%20as,8). He established a mosque (Masjid an-Nabawi) which acted as community center, court, and sanctuary. The concept of **bay’ah** (pledge of allegiance) was instituted: key figures and tribe representatives swore loyalty to Muhammad’s leadership. 

**New Alliances:** Beyond Medina’s city limits, Muhammad sought alliances with surrounding Bedouin tribes. This was partly defensive – to secure Medina from all sides in case of Meccan reprisal – and partly expansion of influence. Many nomadic or semi-nomadic groups were willing to ally with the rising Medinan community for pragmatic reasons: the Muslims were now a force to reckon with, and alliance meant inclusion in the spoils of war and protection. Over time, **mutual aid pacts** were concluded with tribes around the Hejaz[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=Muhammad%20expelled%20from%20Medina%20the,43). This extended the ummah’s network and gave Islam reach into the deserts, setting conditions for later expansion.

**UTC Analysis – Movement to Proto-State:** The shift to Medina can be seen as the Islamic movement **upscaling its complexity level**. It evolved from a tight-knit *cell* persecuted in Mecca (high internal cohesion, high external pressure) to a *coalitional network* managing multiple internal subgroups (Muhājirūn, Anṣār, Jewish tribes, Bedouin allies). The system had to balance **cooperation and competition** in new ways. Internally, cooperation was emphasized (brotherhood, sharing wealth, following a single leader) to maintain unity among diverse members. Externally, competition took the form of strategic raids and battles against Mecca and its allies. Muhammad’s leadership role became that of a **central node handling information and decision flows** – effectively the executive of a budding state. The **institutional grammar** mentioned in the thesis – moral universalism plus sanctified violence – was being codified. The universal moral claims (one God for all, one community transcending tribe, ethical norms) were upheld in Medina as the unifying ideology. The contingent political violence (raids, battles) was now **sanctified** by being linked to divine permission and higher purpose.

In Medina, the Prophet’s dual identity as **spiritual guide and political leader** was cemented. This duality created an enduring template in Islam: the idea that the religious and political spheres are intertwined (the Prophet as head of state, lawgiver and military commander all in one). This **Medinan model** – sometimes termed **the Prophetic State** – would become an idealized reference for later Muslim theorists (e.g., Islamists in modern times who seek to emulate the Medina period as a model Islamic society). But as we shall see, it also laid the groundwork for internal contradictions, especially once the use of coercion – however sanctified – became normalized.

## The Moral Rupture: Badr and the Violence Threshold

The **Battle of Badr** in 624 CE was the first major armed confrontation between the Muslims of Medina and the Quraysh of Mecca. It stands as a **watershed moment** – a decisive break from the past and a “threshold” crossing in terms of applying violence as part of the Islamic movement. Badr’s outcome not only altered the political balance but also introduced an enduring theological narrative around warfare, victory, and divine favor that had profound effects on Islamic doctrine.

**Background and Outcome of Badr:** In early 624, Muhammad received intelligence of a rich Meccan caravan (led by Abū Sufyān) passing near Medina on its way back from Syria. Seizing the opportunity, he mustered about 300 Muslims to intercept it[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=Muhammad%20ordered%20a%20number%20of,he%20had%20a%20dream%20in)[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=Quraysh%20would%20have%20to%20fight,unlike%20the%20Meccan%20verses%2C%20dealt). By this time, several small raids had occurred, including one at Nakhla where blood had been shed, but those were minor. This was the first *large-scale* operation he led. The Muslims marched to a well near the village of **Badr**, hoping to ambush the caravan. Abū Sufyān, however, got wind of this and diverted the caravan. Meanwhile, the Quraysh in Mecca, furious at the Muslims’ audacity and concerned to protect their trade, sent out a **relief force of nearly 1000 men** to confront Muhammad’s fighters[historyguild.org](https://historyguild.org/muhammad-and-the-rise-of-islam/?srsltid=AfmBOoqMOx6jh_4-r7IFAfZibjNQdP-6pVTOBUih7n2zdszVGDZtnH_R#:~:text=In%20March%20624%2C%20Muhammad%20led,Badr%2C%20but%20a%20Meccan%20force). 

At Badr, against odds of roughly 3:1, the Muslims won a stunning victory[historyguild.org](https://historyguild.org/muhammad-and-the-rise-of-islam/?srsltid=AfmBOoqMOx6jh_4-r7IFAfZibjNQdP-6pVTOBUih7n2zdszVGDZtnH_R#:~:text=match%20at%20L615%20intervened%20and,The). About 45 Meccans were killed (including key chiefs like Abū Jahl) and 70 captured, for the loss of around 14 Muslims[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=Quraysh%20would%20have%20to%20fight,unlike%20the%20Meccan%20verses%2C%20dealt)[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=187%20,193). This was not just a military win but a psychological and symbolic triumph of almost immeasurable importance. Muhammad and his followers interpreted it as a **miraculous validation** of their faith: an outnumbered, out-armed force had defeated the elite warriors of the Quraysh. The Quranic commentary on Badr asserts that God sent down **angels to assist** the believers[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=outnumbered%20more%20than%20three%20to,189)[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=192%20,unlike%20the%20Meccan%20verses%2C%20dealt). Belief in that intervention became part of Muslim piety – a sign that when fighting for God’s cause (jihād fī sabīl Allāh), **divine aid** would supplement human efforts. Badr thus entered the Muslim imagination as a proof that *righteous violence in defense of the community is blessed by God.* 

**Violence-as-Doctrine:** Before Badr, Muslims had been permitted to fight defensively; after Badr, **fighting became enshrined as almost a rite of passage for the ummah.** Suras revealed in the wake of Badr (such as Sūrat al-Anfāl, “The Spoils [of War]”) deal extensively with issues of warfare: dividing war booty, treatment of captives, and the ethics of war[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=192%20,193)[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=of%20angels,193). This formalized **jihad** as part of Islamic praxis. While the Quran always frames fighting as either in self-defense or to stop persecution, the community’s self-conception shifted – they were now **an army of God** as well as a congregation of God. The introduction of organized, sanctioned violence created a **moral contradiction** that has been debated ever since: How does one reconcile the Quran’s universal moral calls (mercy, justice, “no compulsion in religion”) with the fact that Islam’s expansion and survival at times depended on lethal force?

Badr, in effect, answered that question for the early community by **sacralizing the use of force** under specific conditions. Success at Badr was quickly attributed to moral purity and divine favor: Muslims saw it as God *fulfilling His promise* that the persecuted would triumph[historyguild.org](https://historyguild.org/muhammad-and-the-rise-of-islam/?srsltid=AfmBOoqMOx6jh_4-r7IFAfZibjNQdP-6pVTOBUih7n2zdszVGDZtnH_R#:~:text=intervened%20and%20the%20Battle%20of,The). This established a precedent that military victory = divine endorsement (an idea that would later become double-edged when defeats occurred, prompting soul-searching about moral failings). It’s important to stress how **shocking** Badr’s success must have seemed at the time. Arabia had not seen a pan-tribal religious army like this. The victory emboldened many fence-sitters to join Islam, seeing it as the winning side[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=The%20victory%20strengthened%20Muhammad%27s%20position,196). Simultaneously, it hardened the resolve of opponents who felt their honor was at stake. The **cycle of violence** had truly begun, and it would escalate.

**Moral Rupture and UTC Framing:** Badr can be viewed as the **point of no return** where the Islamic community crossed a threshold from *principled restraint* to *assertive force*. Using UTC language, pre-Badr Islam was heavily tilted toward **cooperation internally and avoidance externally**, whereas post-Badr Islam accepted **competitive conflict (war) as part of its repertoire**. The “moral rupture” refers to the discomforting fact that a religion preaching high ethical standards now had to grapple with killing and warfare as acts carried out by the Prophet himself. The Quran and later commentary attempted to contain this rupture by framing Islamic violence as fundamentally **reactive and limited**: fighting those who fight you, not transgressing limits, seeking peace if the enemy sues for peace, etc. (Qur’an 2:190-193, 8:61). But inevitably, the logic of warfare began to affect the community’s trajectory: success in battle brought **wealth (booty)**, which needed distributing; it also brought **political clout**, which raised new questions (what to do with the defeated, how to incorporate new tribes, etc.).

From a strategic perspective, Badr put **Mecca on notice** that the Muslim state in Medina was a serious threat. It also meant **no turning back** for the Muslims: the blood of kin had been shed. Many of those killed at Badr were relatives of Muslims (for example, one of Muhammad’s uncles, ʿAbbas, fought on the Meccan side and was captured). The war was now personal and communal. In terms of **legitimacy**, Badr hugely boosted Muhammad’s stature. Even the skeptical Medinese, who had not yet converted, saw that aligning with Muhammad brought victory and plunder. The defeat discredited the Meccan elites’ aura of invincibility and eroded their economic security (caravan trade now under existential threat).

**Counterfactual Alternatives:** It is worth pondering – as historians and moral philosophers have – whether Muhammad had other viable choices besides crossing this violence threshold. Could the Muslims have pursued **economic diplomacy** instead? For instance, might they have negotiated safe access to trade routes or established a market rival to Mecca rather than raiding? At that juncture, it seems unlikely: Mecca’s stance toward Muhammad was implacably hostile, and no negotiation was offered. What about **passive resistance** – could the Muslims have survived and spread by non-violent means alone? Given the small numbers and aggression they faced, this might have led to their annihilation or marginalization (unlike Christianity which spread under a powerful Roman pax, the Muslims were a tiny state surrounded by enemies; pure pacifism risked extinction). 

Another intriguing alternative: an alliance or **realignment with Aksum (Abyssinia)**. Earlier, some Muslims had found refuge in the Christian Kingdom of Aksum (Ethiopia) during the Meccan persecution. Aksum was a major regional power. One could imagine a scenario where Muhammad might have moved the whole community across the Red Sea or sought Aksumite patronage against the Quraysh. However, this would have put his monotheistic mission under a Christian umbrella, potentially diluting its autonomy. Additionally, Arab tribal followers might not have rallied to a movement seen as foreign-backed. 

In truth, **Medina was the only pragmatic choice** that offered autonomy and a fighting chance (literally) for survival. Badr validated that choice. It did, however, set Islam on a trajectory where the sword and the word went hand in hand. The concept of **jihad** (struggle) now practically included **qitāl** (combat) as a form of worshipful struggle. The next sections will examine how this played out: how Muhammad’s role evolved into that of a warrior-prophet (“The ‘Warlord’ Question”), how internal challenges like the **Jewish tribes** were dealt with, and what happened after Muhammad’s death when the community had to sustain an empire built on these precedents.

## The “Warlord” Question

As Muhammad’s campaigns expanded in the later Medinan period (624–632 CE), a critical historiographical question arises: did Muhammad the gentle moral preacher of Mecca turn into **Muhammad the warlord**? And if so, how do we reconcile the early ideals with the later militaristic actions? This “warlord” question underscores the **contradiction between his original mission and the subsequent military-political leadership** he exercised. Understanding this requires looking at examples of his **later campaigns**, the justifications he offered (often via Qur’anic revelation), and how the tradition has interpreted these actions.

By the mid-620s, Muhammad was not only the spiritual guide of the Muslims but their head of state and top general. He led or authorized a series of expeditions and battles, some defensive, some preemptive, which expanded the territory and influence of the Medinan polity. Three notable examples often cited in discussions of the warlord aspect are **Khaybar (628)**, the **Conquest of Mecca (630)**, and various actions with **Qur’anic justification for violence**.

- **Khaybar (628 CE):** Khaybar was a fortified oasis in the north of Medina, inhabited by Jewish tribes (some of whom had been expelled from Medina earlier, like the Banū Naḍīr). After the Muslims and Quraysh reached a truce in the Treaty of Ḥudaybiyyah (628), Muhammad quickly redirected his forces to Khaybar, perhaps to neutralize a remaining hostile enclave and to gain its wealth. The siege of Khaybar ended with the Jewish fighters surrendering. Several were killed in battle; others were allowed to stay on the land as cultivators under Muslim overlordship (providing half their produce as tax). This was effectively **the first expansionist conquest** outside Medina’s immediate vicinity. It showcased Muhammad in a role very much like a regional conqueror: dividing spoils, arranging terms of subjugation, and eliminating resistance. The episode of Khaybar also has some grim notes in the sources: for instance, the execution of the chief Kināna ibn al-Rabī‘ after he allegedly concealed treasure – this involved torture to extract information about hidden wealth[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=match%20at%20L1651%20the%20treasure,was%20then%20put%20through%20torture). Such incidents feed the “warlord” narrative, highlighting the **ruthlessness** that could occur.

- **Conquest of Mecca (630 CE):** After Quraysh violated the Ḥudaybiyyah truce, Muhammad marched a column of perhaps 10,000 troops to Mecca. This time, facing overwhelming force, the Meccans surrendered with minimal fight. Muhammad entered his hometown as a conqueror but notably declared a **general amnesty** (except for a few individuals who were seen as unrepentant enemies). The idols in the Kaʿbah were destroyed, and Mecca became a Muslim city with surprisingly little bloodshed. This conquest is often pointed out to show that Muhammad was not a wanton warlord: he took Mecca with mercy and forgiveness, fulfilling his mission of eliminating idolatry without a massacre. Nonetheless, it was the culmination of a campaign of military pressure. Quranic verses in this period (for example, Surah 9, At-Tawbah, revealed around this time) are quite stern, commanding the believers to cleanse the sacred precinct of paganism and fight those who had broken treaties until they submit (the infamous “Sword Verse” 9:5) – albeit within a specific context. The conquest of Mecca stands as a complex event: it was a **political masterstroke** and largely peaceful transition, but backed by the implicit threat of violence.

- **Use of Qur’anic Justifications:** Throughout these campaigns, Muhammad would announce revelations that addressed rules of war, punishment of enemies, and so forth. For example, after Badr and other clashes, verses justified retaliation and execution of enemies captured in war if necessary, but also urged clemency on other occasions. The execution of **Banū Qurayẓah** men (after the siege of 627) was later contextualized by some Quranic commentators as falling under the law of dealing with treason in wartime – invoking analogies to Biblical harshness against betrayers[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_Muhammad#:~:text=concern%20to%20Muhammad%20when%20he,31)[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_Muhammad#:~:text=their%20lives%2C%20property%2C%20and%20religion,36). The Quran (33:26) references that God helped Muhammad against the “People of the Book” who aided the Confederates, leading to some being killed and some taken captive – a clear nod to Qurayẓah. Also, Qur’an 8:67-68 implicitly critiques the Muslims for taking prisoners at Badr in exchange for ransom rather than fully subduing the enemy, indicating a **divine approval of uncompromising warfare** at that moment. Such instances illustrate how **theology adapted to exigency**: what started as reluctant fighting became divinely mandated battle aimed at broader objectives.

This evolution left later generations wrestling with an image: was Muhammad a man of peace forced by circumstances into war, or a conqueror who used religious motivation to justify expansion? Traditional Islamic sources emphasize that his resort to force was always **reactive or preventive** – never for personal aggrandizement. They paint a picture of a prophet who *preferred* peace (pointing to Hudaybiyyah treaty, the amnesty at Mecca, letters inviting rulers to Islam rather than attacking them outright)[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_Muhammad#:~:text=concern%20to%20Muhammad%20when%20he,31), but who did not shirk from war when it was necessary to defend the Muslim community or eliminate severe threats. The label “warlord” is thus usually rejected in devout contexts as unfairly pejorative; instead he is called a **statesman and general** following divine command. However, secular historical analysis does acknowledge that by the latter part of his life, Muhammad functioned **indistinguishably from other Arabian chiefs or even imperial founders** in terms of wielding power – with the key difference that he couched everything in religious terms, *and* exercised notably more restraint and principle than typical warlords of his era.

Frame this as the **problem of transitioning from prophet to political-military leader**: It’s perhaps analogous to revolutionary leaders in modern times who start with idealistic movements and then must govern and fight wars (think of someone like George Washington or even Lenin – very different ideologies, but a similar shift from agitator to head of state and commander of armies). For Muhammad, this transition was compressed into a single decade in Medina. The expectations of a prophet (mercy, patience, miracle-working perhaps) had to be balanced with the hard realities of governing (justice, including punitive justice, and security). The Quran and Hadith illustrate that he sometimes faced pushback or discomfort among followers about harsh measures. For example, when he ordered the execution of some enemy combatants or sanctioned assassinations of a few who insulted or threatened the community (like the poet Ka‘b ibn al-Ashraf[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=Back%20in%20Medina%2C%20Ka%27b%20ibn,conversion%20hardships%2C%20persuading%20Ka%27b%20to)[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=after%20producing%20poetry%20that%20mourned,guard.%5B%20209)), there might have been murmurs – hence the need to firmly justify such actions as necessary for the survival of the community and sanctioned by God’s law.

One can use **UTC’s lens** here as well: as the Islamic system scaled, it incorporated **higher-order competition** (actual military conflict). The *internal* moral code had to adapt to a *contextual* moral code where killing was permissible under sanctified conditions. This inherently creates a **contradiction** or tension – universal moral claims (e.g., value of life, forgiveness, equality) versus contingent violence (some people can or even must be killed for the greater moral goal). It’s a durable contradiction that didn’t fully resolve; rather, it became institutionalized in concepts like **jihad** (striving/struggle, often subdivided into greater jihad of self-improvement and lesser jihad of armed struggle) and **sharī‘a** (law) allowances for warfare. In short, Muhammad’s later career bequeathed an example where being God’s representative could mean both **preaching compassion** and **leading battles**. 

In comparative perspective, this dual persona is not unique – Biblical prophets like Moses were also described as leading wars, and many nation-founding figures have a saint/soldier dichotomy. But for Islam, which quickly scaled into a world empire, this legacy became particularly impactful. The next section will delve into one concrete aspect of this: the handling of the **Jewish tribes in Medina**, which is often spotlighted as a case of early internal consolidation via arguably **coercive and morally troubling means**.

## Jewish Tribes and Internal Consolidation

Within Medina and its environs, Muhammad had to consolidate his rule among not only his Muslim followers but also other groups, notably the **Jewish tribes** who were significant players in the oasis. Three Jewish clans in Medina – **Banū Qaynuqāʿ, Banū Naḍīr, and Banū Qurayẓah** – had entered into the Constitution of Medina alliance initially[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Medina#:~:text=settlements%20or%20were%20merely%20unilateral,8)[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Medina#:~:text=This%20constitution%20gave%20the%20Islamic,or), but over time each met a dire fate: expulsion or execution. Analyzing these events is crucial for understanding the **political logic vs. ethical cost** of Muhammad’s state-building.

**Banū Qaynuqāʿ (Expelled, 624 CE):** The Qaynuqāʿ were artisans and traders in Medina, considered the weakest of the three tribes militarily[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=Once%20the%20ransom%20arrangements%20for,Arab%20allies%2C%20but%20the%20Arabs). A few months after Badr, a disturbance occurred between Muslims and Qaynuqāʿ in the marketplace – sources differ on the spark (one story involves an incident of harassment of a Muslim woman and ensuing violence). Whatever the cause, Muhammad moved against Qaynuqāʿ, besieging their quarter. They had been aligned with the Khazraj tribe historically and perhaps resented the Muslims’ rising power. After a two-week siege, the Qaynuqāʿ surrendered without a pitched battle[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=Once%20the%20ransom%20arrangements%20for,Arab%20allies%2C%20but%20the%20Arabs)[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=goldsmith.,201). Muhammad initially **intended to execute the men** of Qaynuqāʿ as punishment (which would have been consistent with harsh ancient norms for what might be considered treason or breach of alliance)[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=Following%20the%20surrender%20of%20the,205). However, the leader of the Khazraj (ʿAbdullāh ibn Ubayy, who had not embraced Islam sincerely and later is noted as the leader of the “hypocrites”) interceded strongly, pleading for mercy for his longtime Jewish allies[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=Following%20the%20surrender%20of%20the,205). In a rare instance of Muhammad yielding to a political request, he spared their lives but **exiled the Qaynuqāʿ** from Medina, confiscating their property[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=Following%20the%20surrender%20of%20the,205). They left northwards, some settling in the oasis of Khaybar. From a political standpoint, this expulsion eliminated a pocket of dissent or potential fifth column after Badr. It also redistributed wealth (their properties and arms) to the Muslims, which was a boon for the treasury. Ethically, it set a precedent of **zero tolerance for disloyalty** within the ummah’s domain.

**Banū Naḍīr (Expelled, 625 CE):** The Naḍīr were a more powerful tribe, and their territory was a fortified suburb of Medina. The catalyst for conflict came the year after Uhud. Muhammad, needing blood-money contributions for a case of accidental killing, approached the Naḍīr for help as allies[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=Amir,that%20he%20had%20received%20a). They appeared to agree, but according to Ibn Ishaq’s account, they secretly plotted to assassinate Muhammad – possibly by dropping a large stone on him from a roof during the meeting[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=match%20at%20L1477%20divine%20revelation,was%20an%20unambiguous%20symbol%20of)[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=divine%20revelation%20of%20a%20planned,was%20an%20unambiguous%20symbol%20of). Muhammad left abruptly, claiming to have received divine warning of their intent[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=match%20at%20L1477%20divine%20revelation,was%20an%20unambiguous%20symbol%20of). He then laid siege to the Naḍīr. The siege lasted around two weeks. The Muslims even cut and burned the Naḍīr’s date palms during the siege to pressure them[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=divine%20revelation%20of%20a%20planned,was%20an%20unambiguous%20symbol%20of)[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=siege%20on%20the%20tribe%3B,was%20an%20unambiguous%20symbol%20of) – a tactic shocking enough that the Quran (59:5) later justified it as sanctioned by God despite normal prohibitions against destroying fruit trees in war. Eventually, the Naḍīr surrendered and were allowed to leave, taking what belongings camels could carry[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=match%20at%20L1481%20declaring%20war,land%20where%20barley%20sprouted%20amongst). They were famous for their wealth (perhaps in land and gold). The Muslims seized their remaining property, which per Qur’anic rule (59:6-7) was treated as **fay’** (booty gained without a battle) – distributed by Muhammad as he saw fit to the community, especially to the poor Muhājirūn. Muhammad personally claimed a tract of Naḍīr land for the state (and his family’s upkeep)[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad#:~:text=declaring%20war%20in%20Arabia.,land%20where%20barley%20sprouted%20amongst). The Naḍīr mostly relocated to Khaybar and Syria. Politically, this was another elimination of a potential internal threat – indeed, leaders of Banū Naḍīr, like Ḥuyayy ibn Akhṭab, went on to fan anti-Muslim alliances, including encouraging the large Confederate alliance that attacked Medina in 627. Ethically, it was **harsh but not genocidal**: exile and dispossession, not massacre. Still, from the Naḍīr’s perspective, a betrayal of the constitutional pact (though Muslims would argue the betrayal was theirs in plotting Muhammad’s death). 

**Banū Qurayẓah (Massacre of Men, 627 CE):** The fate of the Qurayẓah is the most stark and has generated the most controversy historically. During the **Battle of the Trench (Khandaq)** in 627, when Mecca’s Quraysh and numerous allies besieged Medina, the Qurayẓah – who controlled the southern part of Medina’s defenses – allegedly entered into secret negotiations with the enemy. Though the siege failed, the Muslims immediately turned attention to Qurayẓah afterwards, accusing them of **treason** for breaking the pact. The Qurayẓah, who had not directly attacked the Muslims during the siege but also did not support the defense, holed up in their fortress. After a brief siege (some say around 25 days), they surrendered, expecting likely expulsion like the prior tribes. However, the judgment was delegated to Saʿd ibn Muʿādh (leader of the Aws, allies of Qurayẓah). Saʿd, severely wounded in the siege and perhaps feeling vindictive, ruled that **all the adult male Qurayẓah be executed and the women and children enslaved**[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_Muhammad#:~:text=accused%20of%20having%20engaged%20in,35)[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_Muhammad#:~:text=After%20the%20Quray%E1%BA%93ah%20were%20found,and%20fared%20better%20in%20the). Muhammad approved this judgment, which mirrored biblical law for dealing with *besieged cities that betray* (cf. Deuteronomy 20:12-14)[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_Muhammad#:~:text=According%20to%20Norman%20Stillman%2C%20the,39). Estimates in sources say around 600-700 men were executed. This was carried out in Medina’s market trench. The women and kids were divided as booty; some women were taken as wives or concubines by Muslims (e.g., Rayḥāna, who became Muhammad’s concubine, though there are reports she later converted and he offered marriage).

The **political logic** here was one of **total deterrence**: after this, no group within Medina would dare entertain collusion with external enemies. It was internal security consolidation by the most drastic means. It also removed the last non-Muslim power center inside Medina. Economically, the Qurayẓah’s lands (which were fertile) and goods were distributed among Muslims, further strengthening the ummah’s base. 

**Necessary or Corrosive?** Historians are divided on judging these acts. Some argue they were **necessary acts of state** – in a zero-sum environment, the nascent Islamic state could not survive repeated backstabbing from within. Given that each Jewish tribe’s leadership *did* act against Muhammad (or was strongly suspected of doing so) during critical moments, he had cause by Arabian standards to punish them. By comparison with how traitors or rebels were handled by contemporaries (or even by biblical prophets like Moses or Joshua), Muhammad’s actions were not extraordinary for the time[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_Muhammad#:~:text=their%20lives%2C%20property%2C%20and%20religion,36)[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_Muhammad#:~:text=According%20to%20Norman%20Stillman%2C%20the,39). As one scholar noted, “traitors were executed” in Muhammad’s time, as in others[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_Muhammad#:~:text=concern%20to%20Muhammad%20when%20he,31). Apologetic arguments also point out that these tribes were not targeted *because* of their religion per se – indeed, other Jewish communities (e.g., in Khaybar after its surrender) were allowed to live under Muslim suzerainty for years. It was specific political circumstances that led to these outcomes. In fact, later Islamic rule generally protected Jewish (and Christian) lives and property as **dhimmis** (protected subjects)[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_Muhammad#:~:text=the%20relations%20between%20the%20two,36), suggesting that the Medina events were not generalized into a rule of "violence against Jews" broadly, but seen as isolated due to war.

On the other hand, these events *were corrosive to the long-term ethical project* in the sense that they introduced a seam of **religious-communal violence** that critics would later seize upon. The slaughter of Banū Qurayẓah, especially, has been a staple of anti-Islamic polemics from medieval times to present, casting Muhammad as cruel and intolerant[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_Muhammad#:~:text=Norman%20Geisler%20%20accuses%20Muhammad,29)[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_Muhammad#:~:text=treatment%20of%20the%20Jewish%20tribes,35). Within the Islamic tradition, while generally justified, the incident also raised theological discomfort – why such bloodshed under a prophet known as “Mercy to the worlds”? Some modernist Muslim writers have even tried to challenge the historicity of the Qurayẓah massacre (e.g., Barakat Ahmad and W.N. Arafat argued it might be exaggerated or only leaders were executed)[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_Muhammad#:~:text=Some%20historians%2C%20such%20as%20W,44). These revisionist views are not mainstream, but they reflect the moral disquiet. Nonetheless, classical Muslim historians accepted the event as factual and saw it as *divinely ordained justice*. The **long-term impact** on Islamic ethics is the acceptance that under certain conditions, extreme measures (including execution or enslavement of defeated groups) are legitimate – a notion that empires following Muhammad did act upon at times.

From the vantage point of the nascent empire, the removal of these tribes did achieve short-term stability. After 627, Medina was homogeneously Muslim-led, and no internal revolt happened. The wealth acquired helped finance the expanding state (e.g., armor, weapons, lands for cultivation). But one could argue it **corroded the moral capital** Muhammad had among some observers – for instance, it likely deepened the rift with any sympathetic Jews or perhaps the Christian view of him in Arabia (though at that time, those were limited concerns compared to survival).

In summary, Muhammad’s dealing with the Jewish tribes was guided by **security imperatives** of consolidation. It was, in Machiavellian terms, effective statecraft: unify your base, neutralize internal threats, and reap economic benefits to fund further growth. The contradiction is that the man who at one time sought cordial relations and even theological common ground with Medina’s Jews (“People of the Book” are addressed respectfully in early verses) ended up effectively eradicating Jewish presence in Medina. To followers, the blame lay with the tribes for their treachery; to critics, it showed Muhammad’s mercy had limits when his authority was on the line.

This pattern – of initial idealistic pluralism giving way to realpolitik – is not unique to Islam’s founder, but its documentation in Islamic sources makes it a permanent feature of discourse. It planted the seed of a certain **hard-edged pragmatism** in Islamic governance: the ummah’s survival and unity could justify severe action. Later caliphates would invoke similar logic in dealing with rebels or disfavored groups (with varying degrees of justice or oppression). The ethical tension remains part of the “purity vs scale” debate: to scale up the Islamic community into a state and empire, compromises (or hard choices) were made that strained the early purity of the message.

## Succession Failure: The Alexander Problem

When Muhammad died in 632 CE without a clear, publicly designated successor, he left his community in a precarious position – a situation sometimes likened to **“the Alexander problem.”** Alexander the Great forged a vast empire but died young without appointing an heir capable of holding it together, leading to fragmentation and conflict. Similarly, the Islamic state Muhammad built was held together by his personal leadership and the unity of purpose he instilled. With him gone, the question of who should lead (and how) became an acute crisis, resulting in disputes that shattered any illusion of seamless unity. This **succession failure** had profound effects: it led to the tumultuous period of the **Rāshidūn (Rightly-Guided) Caliphs**, civil wars, assassinations, and eventually the rise of the Umayyad dynasty – a monarchy far from the original ideal of the Prophet’s community.

**No Clear Succession Plan:** Sunni and Shīʿa narratives fundamentally diverge on whether Muhammad actually did indicate a successor. **Sunni Muslims** hold that he did not appoint anyone explicitly, perhaps by wisdom leaving it to the community’s consensus (shūrā) to decide[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Succession_to_Muhammad#:~:text=early%20Islamic%20history%20and%20the,known%20as%20the%20Rashidun%20caliphs)[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Succession_to_Muhammad#:~:text=successor%2C%20leaving%20the%20choice%20of,known%20as%20the%20%20246). **Shīʿa Muslims** believe that he designated his cousin/son-in-law **‘Alī ibn Abī Ṭālib** – notably at **Ghadīr Khumm** where he said, “For whomever I am mawla (patron/master), Ali is his mawla”[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Succession_to_Muhammad#:~:text=caliphs%20). However, at the Prophet’s death, whatever he may have wanted was not unambiguously known or universally acknowledged. Muhammad had been gravely ill for days, and although there are accounts he hinted that Abu Bakr should lead prayers in his stead (a sign to some Sunnis), there was no formal political bequest.

This lack of a transparent plan led to the **Saqīfah meeting** within hours of Muhammad’s death. While Ali and the Prophet’s family handled the burial, a group of Medinan Muslims (Anṣār) gathered at the Saqīfah portico to discuss leadership. Hearing of this, Umar and Abu Bakr (close companions of Muhammad, from Quraysh) rushed there. In a heated discussion, Abu Bakr managed to secure the pledge of Umar and others, and then general acclamation, as the first **Caliph (Khalīfa)** – meaning successor (to the Prophet’s temporal leadership)[courses.lumenlearning.com](https://courses.lumenlearning.com/suny-hccc-worldcivilization/chapter/muhammads-successors/#:~:text=With%20Muhammad%E2%80%99s%20death%20in%20632,eventually%20become%20the%20fourth%20Sunni)[courses.lumenlearning.com](https://courses.lumenlearning.com/suny-hccc-worldcivilization/chapter/muhammads-successors/#:~:text=Sunni%20Muslims%20believe%20and%20confirm,the%20first%20caliph%20of%20Islam). This process, arguably hasty and excluding some key players, planted the seed of **division**. One faction believed Ali, as the Prophet’s kin and one of the earliest supporters, was bypassed unjustly. Another believed the community chose rightly the most experienced elder (Abu Bakr). The result was the split into what later became **Sunni (those accepting the caliphs’ legitimacy via community choice)** and **Shīʿa (those holding leadership should have stayed with the Prophet’s family by divine designation)**[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Succession_to_Muhammad#:~:text=these%20divisions%20are%20Sunni%20,244%20was%20Muhammad%27s%20designated%20successor)[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Succession_to_Muhammad#:~:text=successor%2C%20leaving%20the%20choice%20of,known%20as%20the%20%20246). That schism, small at first, widened with time, but immediately it manifested as simmering discontent. Ali himself gave a grudging allegiance to Abu Bakr only after about six months, according to early reports, and relations remained tense especially during the third caliph Uthman’s reign.

**Rāshidūn Crises and Assassinations:** The first four caliphs (Abu Bakr, Umar, Uthman, Ali) are called “Rightly Guided” in Sunni tradition, but their era (632–661) was anything but peaceful. Abu Bakr’s short caliphate (2 years) was consumed by the **Ridda Wars (632–633)** – a series of campaigns against Arabian tribes that apostasized or refused to pay zakāt after Muhammad’s death. Many tribes had pledged loyalty personally to Muhammad; with him gone, they saw no obligation to Medina. Some even followed new prophetic claimants (like Musaylima). Abu Bakr, guided by a fierce will to “preserve the system” Muhammad built, declared these secessions unacceptable[courses.lumenlearning.com](https://courses.lumenlearning.com/suny-hccc-worldcivilization/chapter/muhammads-successors/#:~:text=After%20Muhammad%E2%80%99s%20death%2C%20many%20Arabian,the%20Islamic%20community%20of%20Ummah)[courses.lumenlearning.com](https://courses.lumenlearning.com/suny-hccc-worldcivilization/chapter/muhammads-successors/#:~:text=To%20retain%20the%20cohesion%20of,through%20the%20spread%20of%20Islam). The Ridda Wars were brutal but successful – by 633, Abu Bakr’s forces had subdued the rebels and re-unified Arabia under Medina’s rule[voice7news.tv](https://www.voice7news.tv/special/news/17210#:~:text=The%20Ridda%20Wars%3A%20How%20the,PBUH). In UTC terms, Abu Bakr worked for **system preservation** aggressively: the overarching system (Islamic state) had to survive at all costs, even if it meant heavy-handed suppression of individual group autonomy. Interestingly, some Companions initially argued against fighting those who merely refused zakāt, but Abu Bakr reportedly said “I will fight whoever differentiates between prayer and zakāt”[courses.lumenlearning.com](https://courses.lumenlearning.com/suny-hccc-worldcivilization/chapter/muhammads-successors/#:~:text=After%20Muhammad%E2%80%99s%20death%2C%20many%20Arabian,the%20Islamic%20community%20of%20Ummah)[courses.lumenlearning.com](https://courses.lumenlearning.com/suny-hccc-worldcivilization/chapter/muhammads-successors/#:~:text=To%20retain%20the%20cohesion%20of,through%20the%20spread%20of%20Islam), equating political disobedience with religious apostasy – a stance that set a tone of central authority.

The next caliph, **Umar (634–644)**, oversaw the great expansion into Byzantine Syria and Sassanid Persia. Umar was known for his strong administration and austere piety, but he too faced challenges: how to integrate vast new lands and peoples, manage the treasury (Bayt al-Māl), and prevent the Arab armies from dissipating into personal fiefs. He kept tight control, establishing a **stipend register (dīwān)** to pay Muslim warriors and forbidding them from seizing land in conquered provinces (land was left to local farmers, with taxes supporting the state). His murder in 644 by a Persian prisoner-of-war (often seen as revenge for conquering Persia) left a power vacuum which led to **Uthman’s election**. Umar had tried to smooth succession by nominating a council of six to choose the next leader, a partial step toward institutionalizing succession. But ultimately, it still boiled down to elite negotiation – and Uthman, from the Umayyad clan, became caliph.

**Uthman (644–656)** faced internal dissent due to accusations of nepotism – he favored his Umayyad relatives with governorships and financial patronage[en.wikipedia.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_Muhammad#:~:text=Norman%20Geisler%20%20accuses%20Muhammad,29). Regional discontent grew (in Egypt, Kufa, Basra), and communication wasn’t as tight as in Muhammad’s small state; the empire was now huge. In 656, rebels from Egypt and Iraq marched to Medina, and in a tragic siege, Uthman was assassinated in his home by Muslim dissidents. This was a profound shock – the community had spilled its own caliph’s blood. It signaled that **ego-driven factionalism** (tribal and clan rivalries, personal ambitions) was overriding the prophetic ideal of unity and shūrā.

**Ali (656–661)** succeeded in this chaos, but his authority was immediately contested by Mu‘āwiyah (governor of Syria and kinsman of Uthman, who demanded vengeance for Uthman’s murder) and by A’isha (the Prophet’s widow) allied with Talha and Zubayr (Companions) in the **First Fitna (Islamic civil war)**. Ali won the Battle of the Camel (656) against A’isha’s faction, but then at Siffin (657) against Mu‘āwiyah, a stalemate led to arbitration that weakened Ali’s position. A faction of Ali’s own supporters, the **Kharijites**, rebelled, deeming Ali sinful for agreeing to arbitration (they thought only God could judge). In 661, a Kharijite assassin killed Ali. 

Thus, within 30 years of Muhammad’s death, **three of the four Rāshidūn caliphs were murdered**. The community that Muhammad had unified was split into parties and sects. Mu‘āwiyah, having the strongest hand, then established the **Umayyad Caliphate**, effectively turning caliphate into a hereditary kingship (his son Yazid inherited in 680)[courses.lumenlearning.com](https://courses.lumenlearning.com/suny-hccc-worldcivilization/chapter/muhammads-successors/#:~:text=The%20followers%20of%20Ali%20later,or%20Byzantine%20rule%2C%20the%20caliphs)[courses.lumenlearning.com](https://courses.lumenlearning.com/suny-hccc-worldcivilization/chapter/muhammads-successors/#:~:text=governor%20%28Sultan%29,Persian%20warfare). This drew further ire from those who held to the principle that leadership should be meritocratic or chosen by the community (including the proto-Shia who supported Ali’s line, and pious groups who later became opposition movements).

Using UTC’s “**system preservation vs ego dominance**” lens: In Muhammad’s absence, the *system* (the ideals, the ummah’s unity) struggled against forces of *ego dominance* (individual or clan interests). Abu Bakr and Umar largely kept ego in check for system’s sake. But Uthman’s era saw perhaps ego (or at least familial interest) creep in, and certainly the Umayyads’ rise is viewed as the triumph of dynastic ego over the earlier egalitarian ethos. Notably, when Mu‘āwiyah instituted hereditary succession, it was seen by many (like the pious grandson of Muhammad, Husayn, and others) as a betrayal of Islamic principles – essentially the community shifting to a worldly monarchy, which led to Husayn’s tragic rebellion and death at Karbala in 680. That event sealed the Sunni-Shia schism in religious memory.

Muhammad’s lack of a succession blueprint meant the governance principles had to be figured out on the fly. No **institution** was set to choose a leader; it fell back on customary Arabian ways (consultation among elites, then allegiance). Some analysts suggest Muhammad might have deliberately left it open, perhaps trusting that the community would find consensus (he had established the concept of shūrā in the Quran) or not wanting to establish a **dynasty** (since prophethood was not hereditary, maybe he didn’t want rule to be, either). Others say perhaps he believed the imminent end of the world (some early Muslims expected the Day of Judgment soon) so long-term succession wasn’t a priority. Or simply, he might have intended Ali to lead spiritually while an administrative leader would naturally emerge – but that’s speculative.

In any case, the immediate result was that **political authority overtook prophetic charisma** as the glue of the ummah, and that authority was contested. Within two generations, the Islamic state went from the Prophet’s charismatic rule to a quasi-secular hereditary empire under the Umayyads. This had huge ramifications: the ideal of a caliph as both spiritual and temporal guide like Muhammad gave way to caliphs as kings (often less pious, focused on realpolitik). This disillusioned many and gave rise to **opposition movements** – the Shia imamate concept (leadership should have stayed with the Prophet’s holy family) and the Kharijite radical egalitarianism (any pious Muslim could lead, and corrupt leaders must be overthrown).

The **Alexander parallel** is apt: a great conqueror/leader’s death leading to fragmented succession fights. But, unlike Alexander’s empire which split permanently, the Islamic empire, despite civil wars, did remain broadly intact under successive dynasties. One might credit the strong **institutional grammar** Muhammad left – shared religion, scripture, language (Arabic spread), and legal framework – which acted as a centripetal force even when leaders fought. In complexity terms, the Islamic system had to reconfigure at a higher scale: it went through a period of **chaotic reorganization** (the Fitna) and then settled into a new stable structure (the Umayyad caliphate). The cost was the loss of the original community’s purity of purpose. 

Thus, Muhammad’s succession failure (or choice to not rigidly dictate succession) solved one problem – it avoided a potentially contentious nomination during his life – but created many others. It left a question that became **the central political dispute in Islamic history**, fueling doctrinal differences (who is legitimate leader?) and recurring civil strife. This tension between **system vs personalities** continued: even later, when Umayyads fell to Abbasids (another revolution in 750), it was partly on claims of restoring justice and prophetic family rights. The pattern of contested legitimacy can be traced to that early moment in 632 when no one authoritative process existed to say “X is the caliph.” The early community had to improvise, and the consequences of that improvisation – disputes, wars, sects – have echoed down the centuries.

## The Early Empires: Why Islam Scaled So Fast

Despite the early internal turmoil, the Islamic polity expanded with **astonishing speed** in the decades after Muhammad’s death. By 750 CE, barely a century after the Hijra, the Islamic Caliphate stretched from the Atlantic coast of North Africa and Spain in the west to the Indus Valley and Central Asia in the east. Understanding **why Islam scaled so fast** involves examining a combination of **military, administrative, and geopolitical factors**:

- **Fervor and Mobilization:** The generation trained by Muhammad (and the next) possessed extraordinary **zeal**. They believed in a divine mission, which no doubt boosted morale and commitment. The concept of **jihad** as a righteous endeavor promised spiritual reward (even paradise for martyrs), making the Muslim armies exceptionally motivated. Many early Muslim warriors were recent tribal converts who, for the first time, had a unifying cause beyond petty tribal raiding. The Islamic state redirected their competitive energies outward – instead of fighting each other, tribes joined under the caliph’s banner to fight common external foes. This had the effect of unifying the fractious Arabian martial talent into one force.

- **Weakness of the Two Superpowers:** The Byzantine (Eastern Roman) and Sassanian Persian Empires had just fought a devastating series of wars between 602 and 628 CE. Both were exhausted militarily, financially drained, and some regions were depopulated or disloyal. Persia, in particular, fell into internal chaos (court intrigues, multiple coups) after 628, leaving it fragmented just as the Muslims invaded in 633. In the west, Byzantium had recovered territory from Persia only to face revolts and the plague. Crucially, **power vacuums** existed on the Empire’s fringes: Syria and Egypt had relatively light Byzantine garrisons and populations unhappy with their rulers, while Persia’s western provinces were vulnerable. The early Muslim commanders brilliantly exploited this. For instance, **Khalid ibn al-Walīd** and others won pivotal battles like **Yarmūk (636)** against the Byzantines and **Qādisiyyah (636)** against the Persians, shattering imperial defenses. Once those empire’s field armies were defeated, their provinces – long oppressed by heavy taxes and religious persecutions – often offered only minimal resistance.

- **Efficient Military Strategy:** The Rashidun Caliphate’s forces were relatively small but highly mobile (mostly cavalry and camel-mounted infantry who could move quickly across deserts). They often bypassed heavily fortified cities to strike at strategic points, cutting off supply lines (e.g., Muslim forces flanked Byzantine strongholds by traversing seemingly impassable deserts). They were also adaptable, sometimes integrating local fighters. The early leaders like Khalid were tactically astute, using feints, rapid flanking movements, and concentration of force at decisive points. The Muslims also were less encumbered by supply trains – they could sustain on austere rations and local procurement, giving them agility.

- **Administrative Pragmatism:** Upon conquering new lands, the Muslims generally **retained much of the existing administrative apparatus**. For instance, in Syria and Egypt, they kept the tax collection systems and many local administrators (who were often Christian) in place. Rather than try to Arabize or Islamize everything overnight, they were pragmatic: locals were designated **dhimmi** (protected people) if they were People of the Book (Jews, Christians, later extended to Zoroastrians in Persia, etc.), and allowed to continue practicing their faith in exchange for paying a **jizya** poll tax[courses.lumenlearning.com](https://courses.lumenlearning.com/suny-hccc-worldcivilization/chapter/muhammads-successors/#:~:text=overcome%20the%20other%20claimants%20to,Persian%20warfare). Importantly, that tax was often *lower* or at least not heavier than what Byzantine or Persian rulers had been exacting[courses.lumenlearning.com](https://courses.lumenlearning.com/suny-hccc-worldcivilization/chapter/muhammads-successors/#:~:text=overcome%20the%20other%20claimants%20to,Persian%20warfare). For many peasants and urban dwellers, Muslim rule felt lighter: for example, the **abolition of previous emperor’s estates or church taxes** in some cases, or relief from sectarian persecution. In Egypt, Monophysite Coptic Christians had been persecuted by Chalcedonian Orthodox Byzantines – the Arabs were seen as more tolerant by comparison, sometimes even welcomed. The Muslims also usually didn’t force conversion. Conversion was actually slow for the first century; the Muslim conquerors formed a ruling elite in garrison cities, but majority populations remained non-Muslim for generations. This tolerance meant minimal social disruption, making it easier to govern large populations. 

- **Economic Incentives and Tax Policy:** The caliphate instituted a relatively fair and uniform system of taxation: typically, Muslim Arabs paid **zakāt/ṣadaqa** (charity tax) and land taxes if they held land; non-Muslims paid **jizya** and kharāj (land tax on conquered land). Early on, the rulers took care not to overburden – Umar, for example, famously instructed his governors to consider the welfare of the taxpayers. As mentioned, in many cases the taxes were actually *lighter* than before[courses.lumenlearning.com](https://courses.lumenlearning.com/suny-hccc-worldcivilization/chapter/muhammads-successors/#:~:text=governor%20%28Sultan%29,Persian%20warfare). Also, the Muslim state, by not being entrenched aristocracy yet, had less institutional corruption initially – tax revenues were often redistributed as stipends to the Arab troops (which incentivized those troops to keep expanding, to get more revenue). The policy that Arabs were not to seize local agricultural lands but instead live in separate **garrison cities** (like Kufa, Basra, Fustat, Kairouan) helped maintain agricultural productivity and reduced resentment (local farmers kept working their land and just paid taxes, instead of being evicted).

- **Legal and Cultural Unification:** Islam provided a **common legal framework (Sharia)** and increasingly Arabic became the administrative language (though this occurred more in the late 7th century under Abd al-Malik). A unified coinage and measurements were gradually introduced. This facilitated trade across the vast empire – a merchant could travel from Spain to Central Asia under one state, which boosted economic integration. The early empire also, somewhat surprisingly, utilized many talents from conquered peoples – there were Christian Arab tribes as allies, Persian bureaucrats (in Iraq, many administrators continued under Muslim governors), etc., which helped manage the large territories.

- **Power Vacuum & Message Appeal:** In some areas, particularly among tribal societies in the Levant and Mesopotamia, Islamic rule was essentially exchanging one overlord for another, but the new overlord shared a kind of kinship (Arabs who had a related culture/language). The Arab tribes in Syria and Iraq that had been under Byzantine/Persian rule now preferred to throw in their lot with fellow Arabs from Arabia, especially as Islam eroded some tribal feuds and gave a grand purpose. The religion itself – preaching justice, unity, and salvation – had an **appeal** that eventually led many to convert (though slowly). Conversion often brought social mobility; for instance, local converts (mawālī) could join the administrative and intellectual life of the empire, though initially they weren’t on equal footing with Arab tribesmen, causing some tensions later (the “mawālī problem”).

All these factors created a nearly **ideal expansion scenario**: a motivated core, weak adversaries, a flexible and inclusive governance approach, and economic policies that often benefitted the conquered (or at least, didn’t make life worse). Consider the timeline: 

- 632–634: Ridda wars unify Arabia[courses.lumenlearning.com](https://courses.lumenlearning.com/suny-hccc-worldcivilization/chapter/muhammads-successors/#:~:text=,through%20the%20spread%20of%20Islam).
- 636: Yarmūk (Byzantine army crushed) and Qādisiyyah (Persian army crushed).
- 637: Muslim forces take Ctesiphon (Persian capital).
- 640-642: Muslims take Egypt (capture Alexandria, effectively ending Byzantine rule in Egypt)[courses.lumenlearning.com](https://courses.lumenlearning.com/suny-hccc-worldcivilization/chapter/muhammads-successors/#:~:text=,through%20the%20spread%20of%20Islam).
- Late 640s: Conquest of the rest of the Levant and Persia’s heartland (Nahavand 642 final defeat of main Persian forces).
- 650s: All of Sassanian Persia is under Caliphate (last emperor Yazdegerd III is killed in 651).
- 670s: Arab forces reach Ifriqiya (Eastern Algeria/Tunisia); initial bases in North Africa.
- Early 700s: **Umayyad expansion** – North Africa fully taken by 709, **Spain** invaded in 711 (by 720 most of Iberia under Muslim rule), Eastward, Sindh (in present-day Pakistan) conquered around 711, and attempts into Central Asia and toward India continue.
- By 732, the famous Battle of Tours in France marks the farthest northwestern incursion (Arabs are repelled by the Franks), and by 751 the Battle of Talas in Central Asia marks engagement with Chinese forces (Arabs win that, gaining influence in Central Asia). 

This rapid expansion under the Rāshidūn and early Umayyads is sometimes described as one of the most spectacular in history. It’s notable that it wasn’t just military conquest; it was followed by successful **state-building** that lasted. The early Caliphate was relatively decentralized – governors had autonomy but were kept in check by the central caliph’s authority and by the unifying Arabic-Islamic culture that was developing.

However, scaling up so fast also introduced the **purity vs scale problem**: The purity of Muhammad’s original community – small, with leaders of unquestionable piety and direct guidance from the Prophet – was inevitably diluted in a vast empire. The early converts and companions now ruled over millions of non-Muslims, huge wealth poured in, and worldly power often corrupts. Under the Umayyads, many pious Muslims complained of the **worldliness and dynasty** of their rulers (some Umayyad caliphs were seen as more interested in luxury and power than religion). The administrative efficiency sometimes came at a cost of the egalitarian ideals: for example, non-Arab converts (mawālī) were often treated as second-class in the early Umayyad period (still paying jizya even if Muslim, until reforms). These issues sparked opposition, culminating in the **Abbasid Revolution (750)** which overthrew the Umayyads by rallying various discontented groups (Shia partisans of Ali’s family, Persian mawali, etc.) under promises of restoring justice.

Yet, even with those challenges, the reason Islam could scale into empire so effectively is largely because Muhammad had provided that **institutional grammar**: a religion that was portable, a sense of universal mission, legal concepts to integrate diverse peoples, and a model of governance (albeit not fully systematized succession, but enough of an idea of consultation and justice to build on). The early caliphs’ administrative foresight (especially Umar’s reforms) built on that grammar to avoid overstretch. They created garrison cities, left local systems intact, and slowly arabized administration only when it made sense (Arabic became official language of bureaucracy only around 697 under Abd al-Malik). 

In summation, the early Islamic empire’s explosive growth was not a fluke of fanaticism alone; it was underpinned by **sound administrative policies, relatively enlightened treatment of subjects for that era, exploitation of a unique historical moment** (weakened adversaries), and the cohesive ideological drive of Islam. As one source notes, under the early caliphs, taxes were lowered, local autonomy increased, religious freedom for Jews and some Christians improved, and decades of prior warfare trauma were alleviated[courses.lumenlearning.com](https://courses.lumenlearning.com/suny-hccc-worldcivilization/chapter/muhammads-successors/#:~:text=governor%20%28Sultan%29,Persian%20warfare). These factors made the new empire **sustainable**, not just expansive. It was only when internal governance faltered (e.g., nepotism, unequal treatment of converts) that cracks showed, something the Abbasids addressed by integrating non-Arabs more (leading to a more cosmopolitan empire).

Thus, Islam scaled fast because it combined the **passion of a faith-based movement** with the **pragmatism of a savvy empire**. This synergy allowed it to convert conquest into durable rule in a way few other conquerors have matched. The challenge then became: how to maintain the original religious “purity” of purpose amid the demands of ruling a vast, multi-ethnic empire – a theme that recurs in Islamic history repeatedly.

## Modern Continuity Model

The legacy of Muhammad’s project – **“moral universalism + sanctified violence”** – has proven to be a defining and persistent feature of Islamic civilization, shaping governance structures and collective identity up to the present. This constitutes a kind of **institutional grammar** that, while interpreted differently across eras, remains recognizable. Let’s break down how this grammar manifests in the modern (and late-medieval to modern) Islamic world, noting elements of **continuity, fragmentation, and revival**.

**Moral Universalism:** From the start, Islam has posited a *universal community* (ummah) bound by common beliefs and ethics, transcending ethnicity and geography. This vision persists strongly. Today, over 1.5 billion Muslims from dozens of nations see themselves, to varying degrees, as part of a single ummah in a spiritual sense. The idea of a **unified moral order under God** continues in concepts like the desire for Sharia-based legal systems, pan-Islamic solidarity movements, and global Islamic charities. The universalism is also evident in modern Islamic discourse on human rights (with an Islamic framing), social justice, and egalitarian ideals – essentially reasserting that Islam’s moral claims (justice, charity, community welfare) apply to all humanity, not just Muslims. For instance, contemporary Muslim thinkers often emphasize how the Prophet’s teachings advocate human dignity and equality, and these are used to critique corrupt governments or global injustices.

**Sanctified Violence (and its institutionalization):** The notion that force can be divinely sanctioned when used in God’s cause also endures, albeit contentiously. In state structures, this was historically embodied in the concept of **jihād** as legitimation for warfare. Pre-modern Islamic empires (Umayyads, Abbasids, Ottomans, etc.) regularly framed their military campaigns as jihad – whether against European crusaders, Mongol invaders, or rival Muslim factions labeled as heretical. Even defensive fights against colonizers in the 19th-20th centuries were often cast as jihad. In modern times, the concept lives on in more controversial forms: e.g., states like Iran under the Islamic Republic maintain that military power is to defend and spread the Islamic Revolution (with quasi-clerical sanction), Saudi Arabia long claimed religious legitimacy for its rule (though its warfare is more by proxy now, it still couches actions in sectarian religious terms sometimes), and non-state actors from Hezbollah to the now-defeated ISIS invoke sanctified violence, citing prophetic precedents of fighting enemies of Islam. 

At the governance level, many Muslim-majority states today weave Islam into their constitutional identity, even if they are nation-states (a concept foreign to Muhammad’s time). For example, the constitutions of Pakistan, Iran, Saudi Arabia, etc., declare Islam the state religion and often include Sharia as a or *the* source of law. This means theoretically the laws (and by extension, law enforcement – the coercive apparatus of the state) are sanctified by alignment with Islamic principles. Even relatively secular states like Egypt or Malaysia give a nod to Sharia in their legal framework. The presence of **Sharia-based penal codes** in some countries (hudud punishments, etc.) is an example of sanctified coercion at a state level (punishments being carried out because God’s law says so).

**Fragmentation (Sunni-Shia and beyond):** However, the **unity of the ummah** in practice fragmented very soon after Muhammad (as we discussed with succession). The **Sunni-Shia split** hardened over centuries, especially after events like Karbala (680) and differences in doctrine about Imams vs Caliphs. Today, Sunni and Shia communities still often see themselves as distinct (sometimes antagonistic) sub-ummah, each claiming continuity with the Prophet’s true vision. This sectarian division has led to episodes of conflict (e.g., the Iran-Iraq war in the 1980s had a sectarian dimension, the current rivalry of Saudi (Sunni kingdom) vs Iran (Shia revolution) heavily colored by sectarian leadership claims). Other sects like the Kharijites are mostly historical (except the Ibadis in Oman, a quiet remnant). Within Sunni Islam, fragmentation is more about political visions now: various schools of law/theology exist (Hanbali, Hanafi, Ashari, Salafi, etc.), and multiple movements (from liberal reformers to puritan Salafis). Yet, *all* of these factions still operate within the institutional grammar set in Muhammad’s time – quoting Quran and Hadith to legitimize positions, acknowledging the dual spiritual-political nature of the Prophet’s legacy even if they emphasize different aspects.

**State vs Movement Tension:** A significant continuity is the **tension between Islam as a governing state ideology vs Islam as a grassroots movement of reform or resistance**. Muhammad’s model blurred those – he was both head of state and movement leader. But in modern history, especially post-colonial era, there’s a push-pull: Should Muslims aspire to recreate a transnational **caliphate** (the ideal of unity and statehood combined) or focus on individual national states?
